This unique property means it can readily switch between protonated and deprotonated states with minimal energetic cost, making it the quintessential general acid/base catalyst. In well-characterized enzyme mechanisms (e.g., serine proteases, phosphatases, carbonic anhydrase), histidine is **specifically designated** as the proton shuttle/donor/acceptor in the catalytic center.<br><br>- **Glutamate (D):** Similar to aspartate, with pKa ~4.1. At physiological pH, it&#x27;s almost always in its deprotonated form. It could plausibly accept a proton but is not the classically assigned proton transfer residue.<br><br>- **Lysine (C):** Has a pKa ~10.5, meaning it is almost always protonated at physiological pH. It&#x27;s rarely used as a catalytic proton donor/acceptor.<br><br>The critical distinction is that histidine&#x27;s near-physiological pKa makes it uniquely suited and **specifically assigned** as the proton donor/acceptor role. 1. An amino acid that is **specifically assigned** the active-site proton donor/acceptor role
2. An amino acid that is chosen **solely because its side chain could plausibly participate** in proton transfer

Let me think about each option:

**Histidine (B):** Histidine is the classic example of an amino acid that is specifically assigned as the proton donor/acceptor role in enzyme active sites. Its imidazole side chain has a pKa near physiological pH (~6.0-7.0), which means it can easily switch between protonated and deprotonated states. This makes it uniquely suited for proton transfer in enzyme catalysis. In many enzyme classes (serine proteases, phosphatases, etc.), histidine is specifically designated as the general acid/base catalyst.

**Aspartate (A):** While aspartate has a carboxylate group that could theoretically accept protons, its pKa is typically around 3.9, which means at physiological pH it&#x27;s almost always deprotonated. While it can participate in proton transfer, it&#x27;s not typically the &quot;specifically assigned&quot; proton donor/acceptor in most enzyme mechanisms.

**Glutamate (D):** Similar to aspartate, glutamate has a carboxylate with pKa ~4.1. It&#x27;s not typically the specifically assigned proton donor/acceptor.

**Lysine (C):** Lysine has a pKa around 10.5, meaning it&#x27;s almost always protonated at physiological pH. It&#x27;s rarely used as a proton donor/acceptor in catalysis.

The key insight here is that histidine is THE amino acid that is specifically assigned the proton donor/acceptor role in enzyme active sites because of its pKa near physiological pH. The imidazole ring can act as both a proton donor and acceptor with minimal energy cost. This is a well-established concept in enzymology - histidine is the classic &quot;general acid/base&quot; catalyst.

The other amino acids (Asp, Glu, Lys) have side chains that could *plausibly* participate in proton transfer (carboxylates can accept protons, amines can donate protons), but their pKa values make them less suitable at physiological pH. They would be chosen &quot;solely because their side chains could plausibly participate&quot; rather than being the specifically assigned role.

So the answer is B. Histidine.
